Rods in flows: the PDE theory of immersed elastic filaments
Dallas Albritton, Laurel Ohm

Abstract
. We investigate a family of curve evolution equations approximating the motion of a Kirchhoff rod immersed in a low Reynolds number fluid. The rod is modeled as a framed curve whose energy consists of the bending energy of the curve and the twisting energy of the frame. The equations we consider may be realized as gradient flows of the rod energy under a certain anisotropic metric coming from resistive force theory. Ultimately, our goal is to provide a comprehensive treatment of the PDE theory of immersed rod dynamics. We begin by analyzing the problem without the frame, in which case the evolution is globally well-posed and solutions asymptotically converge to Euler elasticae. Next, in the planar setting, we demonstrate the existence of large time-periodic solutions forced by intrinsic curvature relevant to undulatory swimming. Finally, the majority of the paper is devoted to the evolution of an immersed Kirchhoff rod in 3D, which involves a strong coupling between the curve and frame. Under a physically reasonable assumption on the curvature, solutions exist globally-in-time and asymptotically converge to rod equilibria.



1.
Introduction

We investigate a family of curve evolution equations approximating the motion of a Kirchhoff rod immersed in a low Reynolds number fluid. These models arise in the study of undulatory swimming
[
36
,
13
,
107
]
, DNA supercoiling
[
22
,
12
,
97
]
, polymer dynamics in fluids
[
29
]
, and pattern selection in the growth of biofilaments
[
109
,
84
]
. While rod equilibria are well studied, the above phenomena are inherently dynamical in nature, and it is important to develop a theory for the physically relevant equations of motion.
One can consider filament evolution equations which incorporate fluid effects at various levels of complexity. In this paper, we give a thorough treatment of a selection of models derived from resistive force theory.

Curve evolutions

We begin with classical elastohydrodynamics. Let
I=[0,1]
or
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. The filament position
\bm{X}:I\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
evolves according to

(1.1)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bm{X}}{\partial t}(s,t)

\displaystyle=-\big({\bf I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s}\big)\big(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda(s,t)\bm{X}_{s}\big)_{s}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{ss}\big|_{s=0,1}

\displaystyle=0\,,\quad(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})\big|_{s=0,1}=0\qquad\text{if }I=[0,1]\,,

or
\bm{X}
is periodic in
s
if
I=\mathbb{T}
. Here the matrix
\big({\bf I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s}\big)
is the resistive force theory (local slender body theory) approximation of the hydrodynamic effects on the filament evolution
[
36
,
43
,
47
,
91
]
, where the shape factor
0\leq\gamma\leq 1
incorporates the anisotropy of the viscous drag on a thin filament. For an infinitely thin filament,
\gamma\to 1
, weighting the drag along the tangential direction of the filament twice as much as the normal directions.
The terms
-(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda(s,t)\bm{X}_{s})_{s}
model the elastic response of the filament according to Euler-Bernoulli beam theory
[
8
,
9
,
39
,
102
,
107
,
108
]
. The filament tension
\lambda(s,t)
serves as a Lagrange multiplier to enforce the local inextensibility constraint
\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert^{2}=1
.
The boundary conditions come from the requirement that the filament is force- and torque-free at its endpoints. It is due to the surrounding low Reynolds number fluid that the evolution is parabolic, whereas the evolution of a non-immersed beam would be hyperbolic.

The system (
1.1
) with
\gamma=0
is known as the
curve-straightening flow
for a (locally) inextensible filament, i.e., the
L^{2}
gradient flow for the filament bending energy

(1.2)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\kappa^{2}\,ds=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\left\lvert\bm{X}_{ss}\right\rvert^{2}\,ds

with the constraint
\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert^{2}=1
. The critical points of (
1.2
) are called the
Euler elasticae

[
19
]
and themselves have a rich history recounted in, for example,
[
62
,
74
]
. The elasticae are the steady states of any gradient flow of (
1.2
), and various flows have been proposed
[
54
,
53
,
55
,
105
,
106
,
73
]
to understand how the elasticae are attained dynamically. However, the above papers replace the local inextensibility constraint with the non-local constraint that the
total
length of the filament is fixed. Such flows have the same equilibria, without capturing the physical dynamics. On the other hand, the system (
1.1
) in the planar setting
\bm{X}(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is simpler to formulate. In that case, with the inextensibility constraint, the curve-straightening flow has been shown to be globally well-posed in
[
52
,
85
]
for closed loops and free ends, respectively.

In this paper, we interpret (
1.1
) as the gradient flow of the bending energy (
1.2
) with respect to the anisotropic metric

(1.3)

\langle\bm{W},\bm{Y}\rangle_{\gamma}=\int_{I}\left({\bf I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s}\right)^{-1}\bm{W}\cdot\bm{Y}\,ds

defined on perturbations
\bm{W},\bm{Y}
of an inextensible curve
\bm{X}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
We subsequently exploit the connection to geometric flows to prove global well-posedness and convergence to equilibrium for arbitrary solutions to (
1.1
) in both the closed loop and free end settings:

Theorem 1.1
.

The classical elastohydrodynamics model (
1.1
) on
I=\mathbb{T}
and
[0,1]
is globally well-posed in
H^{2}_{s}
, and its solutions converge strongly to Euler elasticae as
t\to+\infty
.

A crucial tool we borrow from geometric flows is the
Lojasiewicz inequality
, which we prove for both curves and rods in Section
6
.

An important application of (
1.1
) is to the investigation of undulatory swimming at low Reynolds number
[
18
,
23
,
24
,
40
,
77
,
59
,
57
,
100
,
60
,
81
,
88
]
. In such applications, we also want to prescribe a time-dependent forcing along the filament to drive its motion. If we restrict the filament to planar deformations only, we may consider the free-end version of (
1.1
) with the addition of forcing by
preferred curvature

\zeta(s,t)
:

(1.4)

\frac{\partial\bm{X}}{\partial t}(s,t)=-\big({\bf I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s}\big)\big(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda(s,t)\bm{X}_{s}-(\zeta\bm{e}_{\rm n})_{s}\big)_{s}\,,\quad\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert^{2}=1\,.

Here
\bm{e}_{\rm n}(s,t)=\bm{X}_{s}^{\perp}
is the unique (up to sign) in-plane unit normal vector to the filament
\bm{X}(s,t)
. Integrating (
1.4
) in arc length, we obtain a formal expression for the center-of-mass velocity of the filament:

(1.5)

\bm{V}(t):=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\partial\bm{X}}{\partial t}(s,t)\,ds=-\gamma\int_{0}^{1}\bm{X}_{s}\big(\bm{X}_{s}\cdot(\bm{X}_{ss}-\zeta\bm{e}_{\rm n})_{ss}-\lambda_{s}\big)\,ds\,.

In particular, the drag anisotropy
\gamma>0
is necessary for the filament to actually swim in a viscous environment. A time-periodic preferred curvature is typically used to model the actuation of a flexible flagellum. The resulting time-periodic solution models the stroke.

In
[
81
]
, the second author and collaborator demonstrated local well-posedness for (
1.4
) and the existence of a unique time-periodic solution given a sufficiently small time-periodic preferred curvature. They subsequently analyzed and performed a numerical optimization on the swimming speed (
1.5
). The gradient flow structure, however, was not exploited. Here we use this structure to demonstrate the existence of time-periodic solutions given arbitrarily large time-periodic forcing

(1.6)

\zeta\in C(\mathbb{T}_{T};H^{1}_{s})\,,\quad\partial_{t}\zeta\in C(\mathbb{T}_{T};L^{2}_{s})\,,

where
\mathbb{T}_{T}=\mathbb{R}/T\mathbb{Z}
is the
T
-periodic interval.

Theorem 1.2
.

Given
\zeta
satisfying (
1.6
), there exists a solution to (
1.4
) in the free end setting whose curvature
\kappa=\bm{X}_{ss}\cdot\bm{e}_{\rm n}
is
T
-periodic in time.

We emphasize that these solutions are outside of the standard perturbation theory. We exhibit them via a topological fixed point theorem (the Leray-Schauder theorem).

Rod evolutions

An important physical characteristic of a rod is its ability to resist twist about its centerline. This cannot be captured by the curve evolution (
1.1
) alone. We thus adopt Kirchoff rod theory
[
2
,
15
,
56
,
90
]
and introduce an orthonormal frame
(\bm{e}_{\rm t},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})
along the curve. Here
\bm{e}_{\rm t}=\bm{X}_{s}
is the unit tangent vector to the curve and the directors
\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2}
span the directions normal to
\bm{X}
at each point. At each time
t
, the frame satisfies the following ODE in arclength
s
:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{\rm t}\\
\bm{e}_{1}\\
\bm{e}_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\kappa_{1}&\kappa_{2}\\
-\kappa_{1}&0&\kappa_{3}\\
-\kappa_{2}&-\kappa_{3}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bm{e}_{\rm t}\\
\bm{e}_{1}\\
\bm{e}_{2}\end{pmatrix}

with
\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2}=\kappa^{2}(s,t)
, the squared curvature at
s
. The coefficient
\kappa_{3}
corresponds to the twist in the frame along
\bm{X}(s,t)
. The frame
(\bm{e}_{\rm t},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})
is material and evolves along with the curve.

We consider the energy

(1.8)

E[\bm{X},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2}]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}\bigg(\left\lvert\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2}\right\rvert^{2}+\eta\left\lvert\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3}\right\rvert^{2}\bigg)\,ds

over the space of curves
\bm{X}
satisfying the inextensibility constraint
\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert^{2}=1
and orthonormal frames
(\bm{e}_{t},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})
satisfying (
1.7
).
Here
\zeta_{1}(s,t)
and
\zeta_{2}(s,t)
are components of an intrinsic filament curvature and
\zeta_{3}(s,t)
is an intrinsic twist
[
89
,
109
,
84
,
38
]
.
1
1

1

Other forms of forcing are possible, such as those considered in
[
70
,
25
]
, but these still require tracking a material frame alongside the curve.
The material parameter
\eta
corresponds to the ratio between the rod’s resistance to twist and its bending stiffness and, for a thin circular rod, satisfies
\eta=\frac{1}{1+\nu}
, where
0\leq\nu\leq\frac{1}{2}
is Poisson’s ratio for the material
[
12
, p. 195]
,
[
75
,
30
]
.
2
2

2

Some sources
[
90
, Chap. 5.14]
,
[
3
, p. 662]
use
\eta=\frac{1}{2(1+\nu)}
, in which case
\frac{1}{3}\leq\eta\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
For isotropic, incompressible elastic materials,
\nu\approx\frac{1}{2}
; in particular, for typical flagellated microswimmers, the twist-to-bend resistance ratio
\eta
satisfies
\eta<1
.

The coupled rod-frame evolution will be a gradient flow of the energy (
1.8
) with respect to variations in both the curve
and
the frame under the metric

(1.9)

\langle\bm{W},\bm{Y}\rangle_{\gamma}+\langle a,b\rangle_{\alpha}=\langle\bm{W},\bm{Y}\rangle_{\gamma}+\alpha\int_{0}^{1}a(s)b(s)\,ds\,,

where
\langle\bm{W},\bm{Y}\rangle_{\gamma}
is as in (
1.3
) and the scalar-valued
a(s)
parameterizes the in-plane perturbation of the frame in each cross section.
Here
0<\alpha\ll 1
is a rotational friction coefficient (
2.12
) due to the viscous fluid effects. The derivation and physical reasoning for this metric will be discussed in Section
2.2
, where the full system with non-zero
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3}
is displayed. From now on, we will consider
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{2}=\zeta_{3}=0
, in which case the system can be written in terms of the curve
\bm{X}
and the frame twist
\kappa_{3}
as

(1.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{X}

\displaystyle=-({\bf I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s})\big(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}-\eta\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss}\big)_{s}\,,\quad\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert^{2}=1\,,

(1.11)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\kappa_{3}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot(\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})+\frac{\eta}{\alpha}(\kappa_{3})_{ss}\,,

where
the tension
\lambda
satisfies

(1.12)

(1+\gamma)\lambda_{ss}-\left\lvert\bm{X}_{ss}\right\rvert^{2}\lambda=-(4+3\gamma)(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}+\left\lvert\bm{X}_{sss}\right\rvert^{2}-\eta\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot(\bm{X}_{ss}\times\bm{X}_{sss})\,.

A characteristic of the rod system is the strong coupling between the curve and the frame: The right-hand side of the
\kappa_{3}
equation (
1.11
) contains two terms. The term
\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot(\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})
is the twist induced by the motion of the curve. The term
\frac{\eta}{\alpha}(\kappa_{3})_{ss}
captures the diffusion of the twist and arises entirely from the independent degree of freedom of the frame. If we substitute (
1.10
) into (
1.11
), we obtain

(1.13)

\partial_{t}\kappa_{3}=\bigg(\frac{\eta}{\alpha}+(\eta-1)|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\bigg)(\kappa_{3})_{ss}+\cdots\,.

Since
\eta<1
, the apparent backward heat in (
1.13
) highlights the strong coupling, and one wonders whether the curve is attempting to decrease its energy by converting bend into twist through some mechanism at small scales. While the twist-bend coupling is known to give rise to large-scale instabilities of rod equilibria
[
32
,
30
,
3
]
,
to our knowledge, no such behavior at small scales has been reported in such a way which would suggest ill-posedness of the equation. In Section
7.2
, we present evidence in favor of unconditional well-posedness. Nevertheless, for the purposes of this paper, we will require a condition on the maximum filament curvature to control the feedback from the coupling.
In particular, we require

(1.14)

\lVert\bm{X}_{ss}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{s}}^{2}<\frac{1}{\alpha}

under which the energy dissipation (where
\gamma=0
for simplicity)

(1.15)

\frac{dE}{dt}=-\int_{I}\big|\big(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}-\eta\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss}\big)_{s}\big|^{2}-\frac{\eta^{2}}{\alpha}\int_{I}(\kappa_{3})_{s}^{2}\,ds

is coercive, that is, controls
\bm{X}_{ssss}
and
(\kappa_{3})_{s}
.

Using the curvature criterion (
1.14
), we obtain conditional well-posedness of the curve-frame evolution in both the free end and closed loop settings. Furthermore, the free-ended filament converges to a straight rod, and the closed loop converges to a rod equilibrium.

Theorem 1.3
.

For
(\bm{X}^{\rm in},\kappa_{3}^{\rm in})\in H^{4}_{s}\times H^{1}_{s}
satisfying (
1.14
), there exists a strong solution to the curve-frame evolution (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) which can be continued while (
1.14
) holds. If

(1.16)

\limsup_{t\to+\infty}\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}^{2}<\frac{1}{\alpha}\,,

then the solution converges to a rod equilibrium as
t\to+\infty
. Finally, there exist
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that (
1.16
) is guaranteed to hold provided

(1.17)

\|\bm{X}_{ss}^{\rm in}\|_{H^{2}_{s}}+\|\kappa_{3}^{\rm in}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\leq\frac{1}{C_{1}}\log\frac{1}{C_{2}\alpha}\,.

Since the rotational friction coefficient
\alpha
is very small, it makes sense to consider the asymptotic behavior of the system (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) as
\alpha\to 0
. As can be seen from (
1.15
),
\kappa_{3}
equilibrates on the fast timescale
\alpha
, and in the limit its evolution becomes quasi-steady, with
\kappa_{3}=\overline{\kappa}_{3}(t)
a function of time only. In the free end setting,
\overline{\kappa}_{3}\equiv 0
, i.e., the material frame is always a Bishop frame
[
5
]
, and the curve evolves via (
1.1
).
In the more interesting closed loop setting, the curve and constant twist
\overline{\kappa}_{3}(t)
satisfy

(1.18)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{X}+\bm{X}_{ssss}

\displaystyle=-({\bf I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s})\big(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}-\eta\overline{\kappa}_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss}\big)_{s}\,,\quad\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert^{2}=1\,,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\overline{\kappa}_{3}

\displaystyle=\eta\overline{\kappa}_{3}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigg(-\left\lvert\bm{X}_{sss}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{X}_{ss}\right\rvert^{4}\bigg)\,ds

(1.19)

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigg(\bm{X}_{ssss}\cdot(\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{sss})+\lambda\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot(\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})\bigg)\,ds\,,

while the tension
\lambda
satisfies

(1.20)

(1+\gamma)\lambda_{ss}-\left\lvert\bm{X}_{ss}\right\rvert^{2}\lambda=-(4+3\gamma)(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}+\left\lvert\bm{X}_{sss}\right\rvert^{2}-\eta\overline{\kappa}_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot(\bm{X}_{ss}\times\bm{X}_{sss})\,.

We show global well-posedness for the system (
1.18
)-(
1.20
) without restriction on
\lVert\bm{X}_{ss}\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{s}}
:

Theorem 1.4
.

The evolution (
1.18
)-(
1.19
) is globally well-posed in
(\bm{X},\overline{\kappa}_{3})\in H^{2}_{s}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Finally, we have reason to suspect that the condition (
1.14
) is not necessary for well-posedness. Specifically, in Section
7.2
, we linearize the rod-frame equations for a closed filament about the multiply-covered circle
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3})=(\overline{\kappa},0,b)
for (large) constant
\overline{\kappa}
and constant twist
b
. We calculate the spectrum of the linearized operator and discover that the strong coupling between the curve and frame evolutions actually serves to cancel out the apparent backward heat in (
1.13
). This suggests that the principal symbol of the evolution
as a system
is elliptic. We intend to exploit this in future work.

1.1.
Additional literature

Steady state classification

Critical points of the bending energy (
1.2
) are the Euler elasticae. The classification of 2D elasticae was known to Euler
[
19
]
. With our boundary conditions, the only solutions in 2D are the straight line, the circle, and the lemniscate (figure eight), whereas the situation in 3D is more complex. The steady state equation is
(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}=0
, and we reproduce the most elementary aspects of its analysis below:

In the free end setting,
\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}=\text{const. }\bm{c}=0
, due to the second boundary condition of (
1.1
). Writing
\bm{X}_{sss}
with respect to the Frenet frame, i.e.,
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3})=(\kappa,0,\tau)
, where
\tau
is the curve torsion, we have

(1.21)

\displaystyle\kappa_{s}\bm{e}_{\rm n}+\tau\kappa\bm{e}_{\rm b}-\kappa^{2}\bm{e}_{\rm t}=\lambda\bm{e}_{\rm t}\,,

from which we obtain
\kappa_{s}=\tau\kappa=0
and
\lambda=-\kappa^{2}
. Thus,
\tau=0
and
\kappa={\rm const.}=0
from the first boundary condition
\bm{X}_{ss}\big|_{s=0,1}=0
. Therefore, the free end elasticae are straight lines.

For a closed filament, the steady state equation written in the Frenet frame is

(1.22)

\displaystyle\lambda_{s}+\frac{3}{2}(\kappa^{2})_{s}

\displaystyle=0

(1.23)

\displaystyle\kappa_{ss}-\tau^{2}\kappa-\kappa^{3}-\lambda\kappa

\displaystyle=0

(1.24)

\displaystyle 2\tau\kappa_{s}+\tau_{s}\kappa

\displaystyle=0\,.

Integrating (
1.22
), we have
\lambda=-\frac{3}{2}\kappa^{2}+q
for constant
q
, which we substitute into (
1.23
). Multiplying (
1.24
) by
\kappa
and integrating, we obtain the celebrated elastica equations for
\kappa
and
\tau
:

(1.25)

\displaystyle\kappa_{ss}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa^{3}-\tau^{2}\kappa-q\kappa=0\,,\quad\tau\kappa^{2}

\displaystyle={\rm const.}\,,

with planar deformations corresponding to
\tau=0
.
3
3

3

The elastica equations (
1.25
) arise also as the steady state equations for classical curve-straightening flow under a global, rather than local, length constraint, albeit with a different constant
q
.

A full classification of 3D solutions to (
1.25
) is due to Langer and Singer in the mid-1980s
[
54
,
53
,
55
]
. The new steady states in 3D include an infinite family of non-planar curves lying on embedded tori of revolution.

The critical points of the full Kirchhoff rod energy (
1.8
) are studied in
[
56
,
46
,
45
,
42
,
26
]
. This classification is yet more complicated, although solutions satisfy
\kappa_{3}={\rm const}
.

Geometric flows.

The most directly relevant references to curve-straightening appear below (
1.2
). Additional analysis results for gradient flows of the bending energy (
1.2
) appear in
[
67
,
66
,
14
]
, while gradient flows for the Kirchhoff rod energy (
1.8
) are studied in
[
68
,
69
]
. These papers are concerned with geometric flows which have the same equilibria as the curve and rod evolutions considered above, without investigating the physical dynamics directly. Interestingly, the flow in
[
68
]
has a quasi-steady
\overline{\kappa}_{3}
evolution like the one we derive in (
1.19
).

We further note that in the non-immersed setting, the Euler-Lagrange equations for the beam and Kirchhoff rod energies are hyperbolic and have been studied in
[
7
,
6
,
11
,
71
]
.

Classical rod instabilities

A rich variety of dynamical instabilities for elastic rods have been identified and studied in both the immersed and non-immersed settings. These include buckling instabilities
[
32
,
34
,
31
,
72
]
, perversions
[
75
,
34
,
109
,
16
]
, bistable helices
[
28
]
, loop and kink formation
[
92
,
35
,
38
,
33
]
, twirling and whirling instabilities
[
50
,
110
,
65
,
34
]
, and Michell’s instability
[
30
,
111
,
76
,
3
,
103
]
. We note that Michell’s instability is particularly relevant here due to the importance of the strong coupling between bending and twisting in the well-posedness theory–see Section
7.2
.

Model justification

We mention further physical justification and applications for the models considered here. First, the Kirchhoff rod energy has been rigorously derived via
\Gamma
-convergence from 3D elasticity in
[
78
,
79
,
96
]
. We also highlight the following articles justifying the form of forcing by preferred curvature considered in (
1.4
):
[
1
,
95
,
93
,
8
]
.
In addition, we mention the numerical analysis results for Kirchhoff rods in
[
86
,
64
,
17
,
3
]
and the convergence of discrete
N
-link rod approximations to the continuous rod in the planar setting
[
80
]
.
Finally, the analysis of (
1.1
) and (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) is part of the second author’s program to understand a full model hierarchy of immersed filament dynamics, and paves the way for analyzing filament models which incorporate a more detailed treatment of the fluid-structure interaction
[
87
]
.

2.
Derivation of the models

2.1.
Resistive force theory

We begin with a brief description of the origins of resistive force theory, i.e., the form of the relationship

(2.1)

\bm{u}=({\bf I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s})\bm{f}

between the force density
\bm{f}(s)
along a slender filament immersed in a viscous fluid and the filament velocity
\bm{u}(s)
.
As a consequence of the highly viscous (Stokesian) nature of the fluid, the relationship between velocity and drag for objects immersed in the fluid is linear. This velocity-drag relationship can be computed explicitly for objects with simple geometries, including slender prolate spheroids with minor-to-major axis ratio
\epsilon

[
10
,
49
,
58
]
. In the slender limit, the asymptotic expressions for the force-velocity relation in the directions normal and tangent, respectively, to the center cross section of the spheroid are

(2.2)

\bm{f}_{\perp}=c_{\perp}\bm{u}_{\perp}\,,\quad\bm{f}_{\parallel}=c_{\parallel}\bm{u}_{\parallel}\,,\qquad c_{\perp}\approx\frac{4\pi\mu}{\log(2/\epsilon)+\frac{1}{2}}\,,\quad c_{\parallel}\approx\frac{2\pi\mu}{\log(2/\epsilon)-\frac{1}{2}}\,,

where
\mu
is the dynamic viscosity. The idea behind resistive force theory
[
36
,
13
,
4
]
is to adopt this relationship pointwise along a curved filament, yielding

(2.3)

\bm{f}(s)=[c_{\perp}({\bf I}-\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s})+c_{\parallel}\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s}]\bm{u}(s)\,.

Taking the drag force to be in balance with the force due to elastic stresses within the filament (typically modeled using Euler-Bernoulli beam theory), we may invert (
2.3
) to obtain, under the time rescaling
t_{\rm new}=c_{\parallel}\,t_{\rm old}
, the expression (
2.1
) relating forces within the filament to the velocity along the filament. In accordance with (
2.2
), the contribution of forces in the tangential direction along
\bm{X}(s)
is weighted
(1+\gamma)
times as much as the normal directions in computing the velocity along the filament, where
\gamma\to 1
for an infinitely slender filament. Resistive force theory with
\gamma=1
can also be derived as the leading order term in nonlocal slender body theory
[
47
,
63
,
48
,
44
]
. The sense in which nonlocal slender body theory approximates the Stokes flow due to a line force density along the length of the filament is made precise in
[
82
,
83
]
.

2.2.
Derivation of the coupled rod–frame evolution

Here we derive equations for the evolution of an immersed rod with intrinsic preferred curvature and twist. As mentioned, these equations, given in the unforced setting by (
1.10
)-(
1.11
), may be considered as the gradient flow of the energy (
1.8
) under the metric (
1.9
). Here we provide the details of from where this metric comes.

Recall that, under the assumptions of Kirchhoff rod theory, we must track not only the evolution of the curve
\bm{X}
but also the evolution of the frame
(\bm{e}_{\rm t},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})
satisfying (
1.7
) along the curve. The general equations describing curve-frame kinematics are

(2.4)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{X}

\displaystyle=\bm{u}\,,\quad\bm{u}_{s}\cdot\bm{X}_{s}=0\,,

(2.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{\rm t}

\displaystyle=\bm{u}_{s}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\bm{e}_{2}\times\bm{u}_{s}}_{=-(\bm{u}_{s}\cdot\bm{e}_{1})\bm{e}_{\rm t}}+\,\omega\bm{e}_{2}

(2.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{2}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{-\bm{e}_{1}\times\bm{u}_{s}}_{=-(\bm{u}_{s}\cdot\bm{e}_{2})\bm{e}_{\rm t}}-\,\omega\bm{e}_{1}\,.

Equations (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) describe the local tilting of the frame in the
\bm{e}_{\rm t}
direction as the centerline moves, along with the in-plane angular velocity
\omega=\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{1}\cdot\bm{e}_{2}=-\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{2}\cdot\bm{e}_{1}
induced by the centerline motion. Using (
1.7
) and (
2.4
)-(
2.7
), we may write the curve evolution in terms of the curvature components and
\kappa_{3}
:

(2.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\cdot\bm{e}_{1}+\bm{X}_{ss}\cdot\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{1}=\bm{u}_{ss}\cdot\bm{e}_{1}+\omega\kappa_{2}

(2.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\kappa_{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\cdot\bm{e}_{2}+\bm{X}_{ss}\cdot\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{2}=\bm{u}_{ss}\cdot\bm{e}_{2}-\omega\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\kappa_{3}

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}(\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{1})\cdot\bm{e}_{2}+(\partial_{s}\bm{e}_{1})\cdot(\partial_{t}\bm{e}_{2})=-(\bm{u}_{s}\cdot\bm{e}_{1})\kappa_{2}+(\bm{u}_{s}\cdot\bm{e}_{2})\kappa_{1}+\partial_{s}\omega

(2.10)

\displaystyle=\bm{u}_{s}\cdot(\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})+\partial_{s}\omega\,.

Here the velocity field
\bm{u}
will be determined via resistive force theory (
2.1
), and the in-plane angular velocity
\omega
is linearly related to the local moment density
M
about the tangent direction of the filament:

(2.11)

\omega(s,t)=\frac{1}{\alpha}M(s,t)\,.

The rotational friction coefficient
0<\alpha\ll 1
is due to viscous effects of the surrounding fluid
[
109
,
70
,
104
,
110
]
. For an immersed filament with finite radius
\epsilon>0
, under our scaling of time, the parameter
\alpha
satisfies

(2.12)

\alpha\approx\frac{4\pi\mu\epsilon^{2}}{c_{\parallel}}\approx 2\epsilon^{2}\log\frac{2}{\epsilon}\,.

It remains to determine the force density
\bm{f}(s,t)
and the (scalar) cross sectional moment density
M(s,t)
along the filament. Recall the form (
1.8
) of the energy
\mathcal{E}
, which we separate into three components as:

(2.13)

\mathcal{E}[\bm{X},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2}]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\bigg(\left\lvert\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2}\right\rvert^{2}+\eta\left\lvert\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3}\right\rvert^{2}\bigg)\,ds=:\mathcal{E}_{1}+\mathcal{E}_{2}+\eta\mathcal{E}_{3}\,.

As in
[
89
,
109
,
84
,
38
]
, we will find
\bm{f}
and
M
by taking the variational derivative of this energy with respect to both the curve
\bm{X}
and the frame
(\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})
.

The configuration space, on which the energy (
2.13
) is defined, is (informally, without making precise the regularity of the curve and frame)

(2.14)

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\{(\bm{X},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})\;\big|\;\bm{X},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}\,,\,|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}=|\bm{e}_{1}|^{2}=|\bm{e}_{2}|^{2}=1\,,

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{e}_{1}=\bm{e}_{2}\,,\,\text{BCs}\}

where the boundary conditions (BCs) are either periodicity (in which case
\bm{X},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
) or the appropriate BCs for a free ended filament. These may include the force- and torque-free conditions

(2.15)

\bm{X}_{sss}\big|_{s=0,1}=0\,,\quad\bm{X}_{ss}\big|_{s=0,1}=0\,,

in which case the corresponding boundary condition for
\kappa_{3}
is
(\kappa_{3})_{s}=0
. We may consider replacing one end with clamped boundary conditions

(2.16)

\bm{X}\big|_{s=0}=0\,,\quad\bm{X}_{s}\big|_{s=0}=\bm{e}_{z}\,,

where
\bm{e}_{z}
is a fixed unit vector which we may take to point along the
z
-axis in a fixed Cartesian coordinate system. The corresponding boundary condition for
\kappa_{3}
at the clamped end is
(\kappa_{3})_{s}=0
, i.e., no twist is allowed to leave through the clamped end.
Here we consider only the force- and torque-free boundary conditions, but note that the clamped conditions are also important in practice.

We consider variations
\bm{X}(s,\varepsilon)
,
\bm{e}_{1}(s,\varepsilon)
,
\bm{e}_{2}(s,\varepsilon)
, which, using the inextensibility constraint and orthonormality of the frame, necessarily satisfy

(2.17)

\displaystyle\bm{X}(s,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\bm{X}(s,0)+\varepsilon\dot{\bm{X}}(s,0)+o(\varepsilon)\,,\quad\dot{\bm{X}}_{s}(s,0)\cdot\bm{X}_{s}(s,0)=0\,,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\bm{e}_{1}(s,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\bm{e}_{1}(s,0)-\varepsilon(\bm{e}_{1}(s,0)\cdot\dot{\bm{X}}_{s}(s,0))\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s,0)+\varepsilon a(s)\bm{e}_{2}(s,0)+o(\varepsilon)\,,

(2.19)

\displaystyle\bm{e}_{2}(s,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\bm{e}_{2}(s,0)-\varepsilon(\bm{e}_{2}(s,0)\cdot\dot{\bm{X}}_{s}(s,0))\bm{e}_{\rm t}(s,0)-\varepsilon a(s)\bm{e}_{1}(s,0)+o(\varepsilon)\,.

Therefore, the tangent space
\mathcal{T}_{(\bm{X},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})}Q
to the configuration space at
(\bm{X},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})
consists precisely of those perturbations
(\dot{\bm{X}},\dot{\bm{e}}_{1},\dot{\bm{e}}_{2})
satisfying

(2.20)

\displaystyle\dot{\bm{X}}_{s}\cdot\bm{X}_{s}=0\,,\quad\dot{\bm{e}}_{1}=-(\bm{e}_{1}\cdot\dot{\bm{X}}_{s})\bm{e}_{\rm t}+a(s)\bm{e}_{2}\,,\quad\dot{\bm{e}}_{2}=-(\bm{e}_{2}\cdot\dot{\bm{X}}_{s})\bm{e}_{\rm t}-a(s)\bm{e}_{1}\,.

It is convenient to parameterize the perturbations to the frame by scalar-valued functions
a(s)
; we may therefore identify the tangent space with

(2.21)

\mathcal{T}_{(\bm{X},\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2})}Q\cong\{(\dot{\bm{X}},a):\dot{\bm{X}}_{s}\cdot\bm{X}_{s}=0\}\,.

In summary, the admissible perturbations are (1) normal perturbations
\dot{\bm{X}}
of the curve, which also ‘tilt’ the frame
\bm{e}_{1},\bm{e}_{2}
in the direction of
\bm{e}_{\rm t}
, and (2) ‘twists’
a
of the frame in the plane of the cross section. The inner product on the tangent space will be

(2.22)

\int_{I}\dot{\bm{X}}\cdot\dot{\bm{Y}}\,ds+\int_{I}a(s)b(s)\,ds\,.

We may calculate

(2.23)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\mathcal{E}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\big((\bm{X}_{ss}+\varepsilon\dot{\bm{X}}_{ss}-\zeta_{1}\bm{e}_{1}-\zeta_{2}\bm{e}_{2})\cdot\bm{e}_{1}\big)^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{I}(\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})\big(\dot{\bm{X}}_{ss}\cdot\bm{e}_{1}+(\bm{X}_{ss}-\zeta_{2}\bm{e}_{2})\cdot\dot{\bm{e}}_{1}\big)\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\bigg(\big[((\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})\bm{e}_{1})_{s}-\lambda_{1}\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{s}\cdot\dot{\bm{X}}+(\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})(\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2})a(s)\bigg)\,ds\,,

where
\lambda_{1}
is a Lagrange multiplier to enforce that
\big[((\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})\bm{e}_{1})_{s}-\lambda_{1}\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{s}
belongs to the tangent space (
2.21
), i.e.
\big[((\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})\bm{e}_{1})_{s}-\lambda_{1}\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{ss}\cdot\bm{X}_{s}=0
. In particular, using the duple notation of (
2.21
), we may write

(2.24)

\nabla\mathcal{E}_{1}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{1}}{\partial\bm{X}}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{1}}{\partial a}=\big(\big[((\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})\bm{e}_{1})_{s}-\lambda_{1}\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{s},0\big)+\big(0,(\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})(\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2})\big)\,.

Similarly,

(2.25)

\nabla\mathcal{E}_{2}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{2}}{\partial\bm{X}}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{2}}{\partial a}=\big(\big[((\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2})\bm{e}_{2})_{s}-\lambda_{2}\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{s},0\big)+\big(0,-(\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})(\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2})\big)\,.

Notice the extra minus sign in the frame component.
Finally,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\mathcal{E}_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{d\varepsilon}\bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}\big(\partial_{s}(\bm{e}_{1}+\varepsilon\dot{\bm{e}}_{1})\cdot(\bm{e}_{2}+\varepsilon\dot{\bm{e}}_{2})-\zeta_{3}\big)^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{I}(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})\big(-(\bm{e}_{1}\cdot\dot{\bm{X}}_{s})\kappa_{2}+\partial_{s}a(s)+(\bm{e}_{2}\cdot\dot{\bm{X}}_{s})\kappa_{1}\big)\,ds

\displaystyle=-\int_{I}\bigg((\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})_{s}a(s)+\big[(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})(-\kappa_{2}\bm{e}_{1}+\kappa_{1}\bm{e}_{2})+\lambda_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{s}\cdot\dot{\bm{X}}\bigg)\,ds\,.

Therefore,

(2.27)

\nabla\mathcal{E}_{3}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{3}}{\partial\bm{X}}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{3}}{\partial a}=\big(\big[(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})(\kappa_{2}\bm{e}_{1}-\kappa_{1}\bm{e}_{2})-\lambda_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{s},0\big)+\big(0,-(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})_{s}\big)\,,

where we note that

(2.28)

\kappa_{2}\bm{e}_{1}-\kappa_{1}\bm{e}_{2}=-\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss}\,.

By the principle of virtual work, we expect the force
\bm{f}(s)
on each filament cross section to be in balance with variations of the energy (
1.8
) with respect to the curve:

(2.29)

\displaystyle-\bm{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{1}}{\partial\bm{X}}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{2}}{\partial\bm{X}}+\eta\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{3}}{\partial\bm{X}}

\displaystyle=\big[(\bm{X}_{ss}-\zeta_{1}\bm{e}_{1}-\zeta_{2}\bm{e}_{2})_{s}-\eta(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss}-(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}+\lambda_{4})\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{s}\,.

Here
\lambda_{4}
is an additional tangential contribution serving to enforce the inextensibility constraint
\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert=1

after
plugging
\bm{f}
into (
2.1
). We may consider the Lagrange multiplier terms collectively as the tension
\lambda
, yielding the velocity expression

(2.30)

\frac{\partial\bm{X}}{\partial t}=\bm{u}=-({\bf I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s})\big[(\bm{X}_{ss}-\zeta_{1}\bm{e}_{1}-\zeta_{2}\bm{e}_{2})_{s}-\eta(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}\big]_{s}\,.

The cross sectional moment density
M

is given by

(2.31)

-M=\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{1}}{\partial a}+\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{2}}{\partial a}+\eta\frac{\partial\mathcal{E}_{3}}{\partial a}=-\eta(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})_{s}\,.

Note that the moment contributions from
\mathcal{E}_{1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
cancel. Recalling the rotational friction coefficient
0<\alpha\ll 1
appearing due to the surrounding viscous fluid (
2.11
), the twist rate density
\omega(s,t)
is given by

(2.32)

\omega=\frac{\eta}{\alpha}(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})_{s}\,.

Using the above expressions (
2.30
) and (
2.32
) for
\bm{u}
and
\omega
, we may calculate the full right-hand side of (
2.8
)-(
2.10
) as

(2.33)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle=-(\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})_{ssss}+(\kappa_{3})_{sss}(\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2})-\eta\kappa_{2}(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})_{sss}+\mathcal{R}_{1}[\kappa_{j},\zeta_{j},\lambda]

(2.34)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\kappa_{2}

\displaystyle=-(\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2})_{ssss}-(\kappa_{3})_{sss}(\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})+\eta\kappa_{1}(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})_{sss}+\mathcal{R}_{2}[\kappa_{j},\zeta_{j},\lambda]

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\kappa_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta}{\alpha}(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})_{ss}+\eta(\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2})(\kappa_{3}-\zeta_{3})_{ss}-\big(\kappa_{1}(\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})+\kappa_{2}(\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2})\big)(\kappa_{3})_{ss}

(2.35)

\displaystyle\qquad-\kappa_{1}(\kappa_{2}-\zeta_{2})_{sss}+\kappa_{2}(\kappa_{1}-\zeta_{1})_{sss}+\mathcal{R}_{3}[\kappa_{j},\zeta_{j},\lambda]\,,

along with the tension equation

(2.36)

(1+\gamma)\lambda_{ss}-(\kappa_{1}^{2}+\kappa_{2}^{2})\lambda=-\mathcal{R}_{\rm t}[\kappa_{j},\zeta_{j}]\,.

The remainder terms
\mathcal{R}_{i}
are recorded in Appendix
B
.

The formulation (
2.33
)-(
2.35
) in terms of
(\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2},\kappa_{3})
is not the cleanest-looking, not is it the most convenient formulation for analysis. We display it here to highlight the complicated effect of forcing by intrinsic curvature and twist, especially in regard to the potential backward heat. We proceed in the absence of intrinsic forcing, in which case (
2.29
) becomes

(2.37)

\bm{f}=-\big(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}-\eta\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss}\big)_{s}\,,

which, combined with (
2.30
), yields the curve evolution equation (
1.10
).

2.3.
Energy balance

To compute the evolution of the energy, we revisit the notion that (
1.10
)-(
1.11
) is a gradient flow of the energy (
2.13
) without preferred curvature, which we simply write as
E
, with respect to the metric in (
1.9
). The abstract theory of gradient flows
\dot{x}=-\nabla_{g}V
tells us that
\dot{V}=-|\nabla_{g}V|_{g}^{2}
. For rods, this is

(2.38)

\frac{dE}{dt}=-\int_{I}\big((\mathbf{I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s})\widetilde{\bm{Z}}_{s}\big)\cdot\widetilde{\bm{Z}}_{s}ds-\frac{\eta^{2}}{\alpha}\int_{I}(\kappa_{3})_{s}^{2}\,ds

where

(2.39)

\widetilde{\bm{Z}}_{s}:=(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}-\eta\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}\,.

While the abstract theory gives (
2.38
) immediately, it is nevertheless instructive to compute
\frac{dE}{dt}
directly: Multiply (
1.10
) by
(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}-\eta\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}
and integrate by parts to obtain

(2.40)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{I}\left\lvert\bm{X}_{ss}\right\rvert^{2}\,ds+\eta\int_{I}\frac{\partial\bm{X}_{s}}{\partial t}\cdot(\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})\,ds

\displaystyle=-\int_{I}\left\lvert(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}-\eta\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}\right\rvert^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle-\gamma\int_{I}\big(\bm{X}_{s}\cdot(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}-\eta\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}\big)^{2}\,ds\,.

Multiply (
1.11
) by
\eta\kappa_{3}
and integrate by parts to obtain

(2.41)

\frac{\eta}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{I}\kappa_{3}^{2}\,ds=\eta\int_{I}\frac{\partial\bm{X}_{s}}{\partial t}\cdot(\kappa_{3}\bm{X}_{s}\times\bm{X}_{ss})\,ds-\frac{\eta^{2}}{\alpha}\int_{I}(\kappa_{3})_{s}^{2}\,ds\,.

Summing (
2.40
) and (
2.41
) and cancelling the terms containing
\frac{\partial\bm{X}_{s}}{\partial_{t}}
yields (
2.38
).

In the case
\eta=0
, the rod-frame energy identity (
2.38
) reduces to the energy identity for the curve-only setting:

(2.42)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{I}\left\lvert\bm{X}_{ss}\right\rvert^{2}\,ds=-\int_{I}\left\lvert(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}\right\rvert^{2}\,ds-\gamma\int_{I}\big(\bm{X}_{s}\cdot(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}\big)^{2}\,ds\,.

3.
Immersed filament evolution without forcing

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.1
for the curve-only evolution (
1.1
) on
I=[0,1]
and
\mathbb{T}
. It will be convenient to consider (
1.1
) as a closed system for the tangent vector
\bm{X}_{s}
:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{s}^{4}\bm{X}_{s}+(\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{ss}+3\gamma[(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})\bm{X}_{s}]_{s}+\gamma(\lambda_{s}\bm{X}_{s})_{s}\,,

(3.2)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(1+\gamma)\lambda_{ss}-|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\lambda+(3\gamma+4)(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}-|\bm{X}_{sss}|^{2}\,,

with zero-stress and zero-moment boundary conditions at the endpoints when
I=[0,1]
:

(3.3)

\bm{X}_{ss},\,\bm{X}_{sss}\big|_{s=0,1}=0\,,\quad\lambda\big|_{s=0,1}=0\,.

The constraint
|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}=1
is propagated by the evolution. To recover the motion of
\bm{X}
, one may integrate the equation (
1.1
) as in (
1.5
)
to obtain the evolution for the center-of-mass:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{I}\bm{X}\,ds

\displaystyle=\gamma\int_{I}(3\bm{X}_{ss}\cdot\bm{X}_{sss}+\lambda_{s})\bm{X}_{s}\,ds\,,

where we have used identities stemming from differentiating the inextensibility constraint
\left\lvert\bm{X}_{s}\right\rvert^{2}=1
to rewrite the right-hand side.
Throughout, we note that
\gamma\geq 0
(with
\gamma\leq 1
for most physically meaningful situations), and we will not track dependence of implicit constants on
\gamma
.

We highlight two key difficulties in obtaining Theorem
1.1
. The system (
3.1
) has a scaling symmetry

(3.5)

(\bm{X}_{s},\lambda)\mapsto(\bm{X}_{s}(\theta s,\theta^{4}t),\theta^{2}\lambda(\theta s,\theta^{4}t))\,,\quad\theta>0\,,

and standard heuristics in nonlinear PDEs would suggest that (
3.1
) should be well-posed in (sub)critical spaces, e.g.,
H^{\sigma}
(
\sigma>1/2
) or
C^{\alpha}
, whose norms do not increase (decrease) when “zooming in” with the symmetry (
3.5
). However, in practice, the need to solve the elliptic equation for the filament tension
\lambda
causes the energy space
\bm{X}_{ss}\in L^{2}_{s}
to act as critical; solving the PDE in the energy space requires the full power of the smoothing. See
[
81
, Remark 2.4]
for additional discussion. The second difficulty, again tied to the tension, is to extract control on
\bm{X}_{ssss}
from the energy dissipation quantity
(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}
. A key insight of
[
52
]
is to separate out the effects of the tension at the level of
\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}
.

3.1.
Linear theory

We begin by summarizing linear estimates which will be used here and throughout the paper.

3.1.1.
Parabolic estimates

Consider the 1D biharmonic heat equation

(3.6)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u+\partial_{s}^{4}u

\displaystyle=f

\displaystyle u\big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=u^{\rm in}\,,

supplemented with clamped boundary conditions when
I=[0,1]
:

(3.7)

u\,,\;\partial_{s}u\big|_{s=0,1}=0\,.

The problem (
3.6
)-(
3.7
) is relevant at the level of
u=\bm{X}_{ss}
.

Let
u^{\rm in}\in L^{2}_{s}
and
f=0
. Then there exists a unique weak solution

(3.8)

u\in C(\overline{\mathbb{R}_{+}};L^{2}_{s})\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{2}_{s}(I\times\mathbb{R}_{+})

which satisfies

(3.9)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|u(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}=-\|\partial_{s}^{2}u\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\,,

thereby yielding global-in-time estimates in the energy space.

If
u^{\rm in}\in H^{2}_{s}
satisfies the boundary conditions (
3.7
), one can multiply (
3.6
) by
\partial_{t}u
to obtain
a priori
estimates “raised” by two spatial derivatives:

(3.10)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\partial_{s}^{2}u\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}=-\|\partial_{t}u\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\,.

If moreover
u^{\rm in}\in H^{4}_{s}
, one can apply
\partial_{t}
to (
3.6
) and multiply by
\partial_{t}u
, etc. These estimates form the basis for a solution theory in Sobolev spaces
H^{2k}_{s}
with appropriate compatibility conditions on
u^{\rm in}
. However, only (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) will be required in this paper.

The energy estimate (
3.9
) can also accomodate a non-zero force

(3.11)

f=f_{0}+\partial_{s}f_{1}+\partial_{ss}f_{2}\,,

(3.12)

f_{0}\in L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{s}\,,\quad f_{1}\in L^{4/3}_{t}L^{2}_{s}\,,\quad f_{2}\in L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}\,,

while (
3.10
) can accomodate
f\in L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}
. We have the following:

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
T>0
and
I=[0,1]
or
\mathbb{T}
. For every
u^{\rm in}\in L^{2}(I)
and
f
satisfying (
3.11
)-(
3.12
) on
I\times(0,T)
, there exists a unique weak solution

(3.13)

u\in L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{2}_{s}(I\times(0,T))

satisfying

(3.14)

\|u-u^{\rm in}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\to 0^{+}\text{ as }t\to 0^{+}\,.

The weak solution is continuous on
[0,T]
with values in
L^{2}_{s}
and satisfies

(3.15)

\|u\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}+\|u\|_{L^{2}_{t}\dot{H}^{2}_{s}}\lesssim\|u^{\rm in}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}+\|f_{0}\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}+\|f_{1}\|_{L^{4/3}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}+\|f_{2}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\,,

where the space-time norms are taken on
I\times(0,T)
.

For every
u^{\rm in}\in H^{2}(I)
(satisfying (
3.7
) when
I=[0,1]
) and
f\in L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}
, the unique weak solution additionally belongs to
C([0,T];H^{2}_{s})\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{4}_{s}
and satisfies

(3.16)

\|\partial_{s}^{2}u\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}+\|\partial_{s}^{2}u\|_{L^{2}_{t}\dot{H}^{2}_{s}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\,.

Remark 3.2

(Solvability at
\bm{X}_{s}
level)
.

The above considerations were at the level of
\bm{X}_{ss}
. However, we often consider the evolution at the level of
\bm{X}_{s}
, for example,

(3.17)

\partial_{t}v+\partial_{s}^{4}v=0\,,\quad v\big|_{t=0}=v^{\rm in}\,,\quad\partial_{s}v\,,\,\partial_{s}^{2}v\big|_{s=0,1}=0\,,

with initial data
v^{\rm in}\in H^{1}(I)
.

To solve (
3.17
), we consider
u^{\rm in}=\partial_{s}v^{\rm in}\in L^{2}
. Let
u
be the solution to the clamped problem (
3.6
)-(
3.7
) with initial data
u^{\rm in}
. This determines
v
up to a constant, which can be recovered by the value of
v
at
s=0
. Define

(3.18)

v(s,t)=\int_{0}^{s}u(s^{\prime},t)\,ds^{\prime}+v^{\rm in}(0)\,.

Then
v
solves (
3.17
), and it belongs to
C([0,T];H^{1}_{s})\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{3}_{s}(I\times(0,T))
.

The construction works the same with a forcing term
f
on the right-hand side in (
3.17
). Then
u
solves the clamped problem with forcing term
\partial_{s}f
, and

(3.19)

v(s,t)=\int_{0}^{s}u(s^{\prime},t)\,ds^{\prime}+v^{\rm in}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}f(0,t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\,.

A suitable requirement is that
f
satisfies (
3.11
)-(
3.12
) with
H^{1}_{s}
instead of
L^{2}_{s}
.

Uniqueness
. Suppose that
\widetilde{v}\in C([0,T];H^{1})\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{3}_{s}(I\times(0,T))
is a second solution with initial data
v^{\rm in}
. Then
\widetilde{u}=\partial_{s}\widetilde{v}
solves the clamped problem (
3.6
)-(
3.7
), and therefore
u-\widetilde{u}
is zero. Hence,
v-\widetilde{v}
is constant-in-space. By the equation
(\partial_{t}+\partial_{s}^{4})(v-\widetilde{v})=0
, it is therefore constant-in-time. Since
(v-\widetilde{v})|_{t=0}=0
, we may conclude that
v\equiv\widetilde{v}
.

3.1.2.
Tension estimates

We next derive bounds for the tension
\lambda
.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\bm{X}_{s}\in H^{3}_{s}
. Then there exists a unique solution
\lambda\in H^{1}_{0}(0,1)
in the free end setting or
\lambda\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
in the closed loop setting to

(3.20)

(1+\gamma)\lambda_{ss}-|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\lambda+(3\gamma+4)(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}-|\bm{X}_{sss}|^{2}=0

satisfying the estimate

(3.21)

\|\lambda\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}^{2}\,.

If additionally
\bm{X}_{s}\in H^{4}_{s}
, then

(3.22)

\|\lambda\|_{H^{2}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{3}+\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\,.

Proof.

From (
3.20
), we have the energy estimate

(3.23)

(1+\gamma)\int_{I}|\lambda_{s}|^{2}\,ds+\int_{I}|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\lambda^{2}\,ds=(\gamma+2)\int_{I}(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})\lambda_{s}\,ds-\int_{I}|P_{\bm{X}_{s}}^{\perp}\bm{X}_{sss}|^{2}\lambda\,ds\,.

The right-hand side may be estimated according to

(3.24)

\int_{I}\big(|\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss}||\lambda_{s}|+|P_{\bm{X}_{s}}^{\perp}\bm{X}_{sss}|^{2}|\lambda|\big)\,ds\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\|\lambda_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}+\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\|\lambda\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\,.

When
I=[0,1]
, both
\lVert\lambda_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}_{s}}
and
\lVert\lambda\rVert_{L^{\infty}_{s}}
may be controlled by the left-hand side term
\int_{0}^{1}|\lambda_{s}|^{2}\,ds
alone. When
I=\mathbb{T}
, it is necessary to also utilize the term
\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\lambda^{2}\,ds
. Specifically, we need to demonstrate that the left-hand side of (
3.23
) controls
\|\lambda\|_{H^{1}_{s}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}
. For this, we exploit
Fenchel’s theorem

[
21
]
:

(3.25)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds\geq\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\bm{X}_{ss}|\,ds\geq 2\pi\,.

For all
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and all non-negative potentials
K
with
\|K\|_{L^{1}}\geq 2\pi
, we have

(3.26)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}|f|^{2}\,ds\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}}K|f|^{2}\,ds+\int_{\mathbb{T}}|f_{s}|^{2}\,ds\,.

The crucial point is to control the mean
m=\int_{\mathbb{T}}f\,ds
. Let
\widetilde{f}=f-m
. Then

(3.27)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}K|m|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}K|f-\widetilde{f}|^{2}\,ds\leq 2K|f|^{2}+2\int_{\mathbb{T}}K|\widetilde{f}|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{T}}K|f|^{2}\,ds+\|\widetilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}K\,ds\,.

In particular,
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
is a Hilbert space with inner product

(3.28)

(f,g)_{*}:=(1+\gamma)\int_{\mathbb{T}}f_{s}g_{s}\,ds+\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}fg\,ds\,.

Then, according to the Riesz representation theorem, the linear PDE

(3.29)

(1+\gamma)\lambda_{ss}-|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\lambda=q

is uniquely solvable in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
for all
q\in(H^{1})^{*}
.

To prove (
3.22
), we view (
3.20
) as an equality for
\lambda_{ss}
, estimate the remaining terms by a constant multiple of

(3.30)

\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{4}}^{2}\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}}+\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{4}}^{2}\,,

and then exploit interpolation. ∎

3.2.
Local well-posedness

To establish global well-posedness for (
3.1
), we begin with local well-posedness and, in the next section, argue that the short-time solution can be continued indefinitely.

Proposition 3.4
.

(Local well-posedness)
Let
\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\in H^{1}_{s}
. Then there exists
T>0
and a unique solution

(3.31)

\bm{X}_{s}\in C([0,T];H^{1}_{s})\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{3}_{s}(I\times(0,T))

to (
3.1
) which depends Lipschitz-continuously on its initial data
\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\in H^{1}_{s}
in the topology of (
3.31
).
4
4

4

The solution is unique in the sense that any two such solutions on time intervals
[0,T_{1}]
,
[0,T_{2}]
agree on their mutual existence time.
For solutions in the above class, if
|\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}|^{2}=1
, then
|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}=1
.

(Blow-up criterion)
Suppose that
\bm{X}_{s}
is the maximally defined solution with maximal time of existence
T^{*}\in(0,+\infty]
. If
T^{*}<+\infty
, then

(3.32)

\|\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}\cap L^{4}_{t}H^{2}_{s}(I\times(0,T^{*}))}=+\infty\,.

(Higher regularity)
If
\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\in H^{3}_{s}
satisfies the boundary conditions, then

(3.33)

\bm{X}_{s}\in C([0,T];H^{3}_{s})\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{5}_{s}(I\times(0,T))\,,\quad\forall T<+\infty\,.

For solutions satisfying (
3.31
), the tension
\lambda
belongs to
L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{s}(I\times(0,T))
.

Proof.

1. Local well-posedness in the energy space
. Let
T>0
. We seek a fixed point of the map
\bm{Y}_{s}\mapsto\bm{X}_{s}
, where

(3.34)

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{s}

\displaystyle=e^{-t\partial_{s}^{4}}\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-t^{\prime})\partial_{s}^{4}}\partial_{s}[(\lambda\bm{Y}_{s})_{s}+\gamma(\lambda_{s}\bm{Y}_{s})]\,dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle+3\gamma\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-t^{\prime})\partial_{s}^{4}}\partial_{s}(\bm{Y}_{sss}\cdot\bm{Y}_{ss})\bm{Y}_{s}\,dt^{\prime}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}[\bm{Y}_{s}]^{-1}\big(|\bm{Y}_{sss}|^{2}-(3\gamma+4)(\bm{Y}_{sss}\cdot\bm{Y}_{ss})_{s}\big)\,,

and
\mathcal{T}[\bm{Y}_{s}]^{-1}(f)
is the solution
\lambda
of the equation

(3.35)

(1+\gamma)\lambda_{ss}-|\bm{Y}_{ss}|^{2}\lambda=f\,.

Here and throughout, the semigroup
e^{-\partial_{s}^{4}}
is understood with the appropriate boundary conditions. We employ the functional setup
5
5

5

We could also choose
\mathcal{Y}_{1}:=L^{2}(0,T;H^{3}_{s})\cap{\rm BCs}
, so that the
\mathcal{Y}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{1}
is the natural energy space.

(3.36)

\mathcal{Y}_{0}=C([0,T];H^{1}_{s})\,,\quad\mathcal{Y}_{1}:=L^{4}(0,T;H^{2}_{s})\cap\{{\rm BCs}\}\,.

Let
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
be upper bounds for
\|\bm{Y}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{0}}
and
\|\bm{Y}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}
, respectively.

The map stabilizes a ball
. We record the tension estimate

(3.37)

\|\lambda\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}^{2}

and the contribution of the initial data,

(3.38)

\|e^{-t\partial_{s}^{4}}\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{0}}+\underbrace{\|e^{-t\partial_{s}^{4}}\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}}_{M^{\rm in}_{1}(T)}\lesssim\underbrace{\|\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\|_{H^{1}}}_{M^{\rm in}_{0}}\,,

keeping in mind that
M^{\rm in}_{1}(T)=o_{T\to 0^{+}}(1)
, since
\|\cdot\|_{L^{4}_{t}H^{2}_{s}(I\times(0,T))}
is an integral quantity.

The main estimates are for the integral terms in (
3.34
). By the energy estimates,

(3.39)

\|\bm{X}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{0}}+\|\bm{X}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}\lesssim\|(\lambda\bm{Y}_{s})_{s}+\gamma(\lambda_{s}\bm{Y}_{s})\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}+\|(\bm{Y}_{sss}\cdot\bm{Y}_{ss})\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\,.

We may then bound

(3.40)

\displaystyle\|(\lambda\bm{Y}_{s})_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}+\|\lambda_{s}\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\lesssim\|\lambda\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim M_{1}^{2}M_{0}

\displaystyle\|(\bm{Y}_{sss}\cdot\bm{Y}_{ss})\cdot\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}_{ss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{\infty}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{1/8}M_{1}^{3/2}M_{0}^{3/2}\,,

where we have used that
\|\bm{Y}_{ss}\|_{L^{8}_{t}L^{\infty}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}
.

In conclusion, we obtain

(3.41)

\displaystyle\|\bm{X}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq M^{\rm in}_{1}(T)+C(M_{1}^{2}M_{0}+T^{1/8}M_{1}^{3/2}M_{0}^{3/2})\quad(\leq 3M_{1}/4)

\displaystyle\|\bm{X}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq CM^{\rm in}_{0}+C(M_{1}^{2}M_{0}+T^{1/8}M_{1}^{3/2}M_{0}^{3/2})\quad(\leq 3M_{0}/4)\,,

provided we make the following choices:
First, choose
M_{0}\geq 4CM^{\rm in}_{0}
. Then, choose
M_{1}\leq\min((4CM_{0})^{-1},(4C)^{-1/2})
. Finally, choose
T\leq 1
small enough to satisfy
CT^{1/8}M_{1}^{3/2}M_{0}^{3/2}\leq\min(M_{0},M_{1})/4
and
M^{\rm in}_{1}(T)\leq M_{1}/4
.

Contraction
. We begin with estimates on the difference between two tensions. For two curves
\bm{Y}^{(1)}
,
\bm{Y}^{(2)}
, we define

(3.42)

R_{\lambda}^{(k)}:=|\bm{Y}^{(k)}_{sss}|^{2}-(3\gamma+4)(\bm{Y}_{sss}^{(k)}\cdot\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(k)})_{s}\,,\quad k=1,2\,,

and the tensions

(3.43)

\lambda^{(k)}=\mathcal{T}[\bm{Y}_{s}^{(k)}]^{-1}(R_{\lambda}^{(k)})\,,\quad k=1,2\,.

The difference
\lambda^{(1)}-\lambda^{(2)}
may then be written

(3.44)

\lambda^{(1)}-\lambda^{(2)}=[\mathcal{T}[\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{s}]^{-1}-\mathcal{T}[\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{s}]^{-1}](R_{\lambda}^{(1)})+\mathcal{T}[\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{s}]^{-1}(R_{\lambda}^{(1)}-R_{\lambda}^{(2)})\,.

First, we estimate

(3.45)

\mathcal{T}[\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{s}]^{-1}(R_{\lambda}^{(1)})-\mathcal{T}[\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{s}]^{-1}(R_{\lambda}^{(1)})=:q^{(1)}-q^{(2)}\,.

Notably,
q^{(1)}=\lambda^{(1)}
. Then

(3.46)

(1+\gamma)\big(q^{(2)}-q^{(1)}\big)_{ss}-|\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(2)}|^{2}q^{(2)}+|\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(1)}|^{2}q^{(1)}=0\,,

which we expand as

(3.47)

(1+\gamma)\big(q^{(2)}-q^{(1)}\big)_{ss}-|\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(2)}|^{2}(q^{(2)}-q^{(1)})-(|\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(2)}|^{2}-|\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(1)}|^{2})q^{(1)}=0

and estimate as

(3.48)

\|q^{(2)}-q^{(1)}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim(\|\bm{Y}_{s}^{(1)}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}+\|\bm{Y}_{s}^{(2)}\|_{H^{1}_{s}})\|\bm{Y}_{s}^{(2)}-\bm{Y}_{s}^{(1)}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\|q^{(1)}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\,.

Second, we estimate

(3.49)

\displaystyle\|R_{\lambda}^{(1)}-R_{\lambda}^{(2)}\|_{(H^{1}_{s})^{*}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}+\|\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\big)\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{sss}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}+\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{sss}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{ss}\|^{1/2}_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{sss}\|^{1/2}_{L^{2}_{s}}

\displaystyle+\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{ss}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{sss}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}\|\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\,.

Altogether, the difference between tensions is estimated by

(3.50)

\displaystyle\|\lambda^{(1)}-\lambda^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim M_{0}M_{1}^{2}\|\bm{Y}_{s}^{(1)}-\bm{Y}_{s}^{(2)}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{0}}+M_{1}\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{s}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}

\displaystyle+\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{s}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}T^{1/8}M_{1}^{1/2}M_{0}^{1/2}+\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{s}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{0}}^{1/2}\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{s}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}}^{1/2}T^{1/4}M_{1}^{3/2}\,.

We now turn to estimating the difference

(3.51)

\displaystyle\bm{X}^{(1)}_{s}-\bm{X}^{(2)}_{s}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-t^{\prime})\partial_{s}^{4}}\partial_{s}R_{\rm t}\,dt^{\prime}+(3\gamma+4)\int_{0}^{t}e^{-(t-t^{\prime})\partial_{s}^{4}}\partial_{s}R_{3}\,dt^{\prime}\,,

where

(3.52)

\displaystyle R_{\rm t}

\displaystyle=\big(\lambda^{(1)}\bm{Y}_{s}^{(1)}\big)_{s}-\big(\lambda^{(2)}\bm{Y}_{s}^{(2)}\big)_{s}+\gamma\lambda_{s}^{(1)}\bm{Y}_{s}^{(1)}-\gamma\lambda^{(2)}_{s}\bm{Y}_{s}^{(2)}\,,

\displaystyle R_{3}

\displaystyle=(\bm{Y}_{sss}^{(1)}\cdot\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(1)})\bm{Y}_{s}^{(1)}-(\bm{Y}_{sss}^{(2)}\cdot\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(2)})\bm{Y}_{s}^{(2)}\,.

These remainder terms may be estimated as

(3.53)

\displaystyle\|R_{\rm t}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\lambda^{(1)}-\lambda^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}M_{0}+M_{1}^{2}\|\bm{Y}_{s}^{(1)}-\bm{Y}_{s}^{(2)}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{0}}\,,

\displaystyle\|R_{3}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{1/8}M_{0}M_{1}\|\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(1)}-\bm{Y}_{ss}^{(2)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}\|\bm{Y}_{sss}^{(1)}-\bm{Y}_{sss}^{(2)}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\|\bm{Y}_{sss}^{(1)}-\bm{Y}_{sss}^{(2)}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}T^{1/8}M_{0}^{3/2}M_{1}^{1/2}+T^{1/8}M_{1}^{3/2}M_{0}^{1/2}\|\bm{Y}_{s}^{(1)}-\bm{Y}_{s}^{(2)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}\,.

By Duhamel’s formula, similar to the estimates (
3.41
), we obtain

(3.54)

\|\bm{X}^{(1)}_{s}-\bm{X}^{(2)}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{0}}\lesssim C(1+T^{1/8})(1+C(M_{0}))o_{M_{1}\to 0^{+}}(1)\|\bm{Y}^{(1)}_{s}-\bm{Y}^{(2)}_{s}\|_{\mathcal{Y}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Y}_{0}}\,,

where
C(M_{0})
is an increasing function of
M_{0}
, and we recall
T\leq 1
. A key component of the above argument is that
M_{0}
does not appear without
M_{1}
. Finally, we can decrease
M_{1}
(depending on
M_{0}
, more specifically, like an inverse power of
M_{0}
) as necessary to ensure that the system is contractive. Notably,
T
can be chosen to depend continuously on
\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\in H^{1}_{s}
.

2. Continuation criterion
. The above construction gives the continuation criterion
\bm{X}_{s}\in C([0,T];H^{1}_{s})
. However, the above estimates on Duhamel’s formula yield that any solution
\bm{X}_{s}\in L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}\cap L^{4}_{t}H^{2}_{s}(I\times(0,T))
belongs to
C([0,T];H^{1}_{s})
.

3. Higher regularity
. We will demonstrate that, on a possibly shorter time interval
(0,T)
, the solution satisfies

(3.55)

\|\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{3}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{5}_{s}(I\times(0,T))}\lesssim\|e^{-t\partial_{s}^{4}}\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{3}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{5}_{s}(I\times(0,T))}\,.

The method is to prove the estimate (
3.55
) for the Picard iterates and pass it to the solution
\bm{X}_{s}
by lower semi-continuity.
6
6

6

A posteriori
, the estimates below with
\bm{Y}_{s}=\bm{X}_{s}
will demonstrate that the solution belongs to
C([0,T];H^{3}_{s})
.
The relevant estimates on Duhamel’s formula (
3.34
) will be derived from the linear estimate (
3.16
). The contribution from the initial data is

(3.56)

\|e^{-t\partial_{s}^{4}}\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{3}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{5}_{s}(I\times(0,T))}\leq C_{0}\|\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\|_{H^{3}_{s}}\,.

The “forces” inside Duhamel’s formula should be estimated in
L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{s}
. For these particular estimates, we divide the terms into “new information”
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{3}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{5}_{s}
(of which we are allowed only one copy) and “old information”
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{3}_{s}
(which we collect in square brackets):
7
7

7

Alternatively, we could close a contraction argument in
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{3}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{5}_{s}
on a time of existence depending on
\|\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\|_{H^{3}_{s}}
.

(3.57)

\displaystyle\|(\bm{Y}_{sss}\cdot\bm{Y}_{ss})\bm{Y}_{s})_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\bm{Y}_{ssss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}\big[\|\bm{Y}_{ss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{1}_{s}}\|\big]

\displaystyle+\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}\big[\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}+\|\bm{Y}_{ss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}^{2}\big]\,.

By the tension estimates (
3.22
), we have

(3.58)

\displaystyle\|\lambda[\bm{Y}_{s}]\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{2}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\big[\|\bm{Y}_{ss}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\big]+\|\bm{Y}_{ssss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\big[\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\big]\,,

\displaystyle\|\lambda[\bm{Y}_{s}]\|_{L^{4}_{t}H^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\big[\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{4}_{t}L^{2}_{s}}\big]\,,

which yield

(3.59)

\|\lambda[\bm{Y}_{s}]\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{t}H^{2}_{s}}\leq\|\bm{Y}_{sss}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{2}_{s}(I\times(0,T))}C(M_{0})o_{M_{1}\to 0^{+}}(1)\,.

In summary, we have

(3.60)

\|\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{3}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{5}_{s}(I\times(0,T))}\leq C_{0}\|\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\|_{H^{3}}+C(M_{0})o_{M_{1}\to 0^{+}}(1)\|\bm{Y}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{2}_{s}(I\times(0,T))}\,.

If we choose
T\ll 1
to ensure that
C(M_{0})o_{M_{1}\to 0^{+}}(1)\leq 1/2
, then the estimate (
3.55
) with the constant
2C_{0}
will be propagated by the Picard iterates. Notably,
T
can still be chosen to depend continuously on
\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\in H^{1}_{s}
.

4. Unit length
.
A posteriori
, we may justify that the condition
|\bm{X}_{s}|=1
is propagated by solutions to the equation. Suppose that
\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\in H^{3}_{s}
with
|\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}|^{2}=1
. We multiply (
3.1
) by
\bm{X}_{s}
and simplify
without
the condition
|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}=1
:

(3.61)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}+\partial_{s}^{4})|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}

\displaystyle=(1+\gamma)\lambda_{ss}(|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}-1)+\frac{1}{2}(1+\gamma)\lambda_{s}\partial_{s}|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\lambda\partial_{s}^{2}|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}

\displaystyle+3\gamma(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})_{s}(|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}-1)+3\gamma(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}\,,

where we have already applied the tension equation (
3.20
). This may be viewed as a parabolic equation for the quantity
|\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}-1
with initial data
|\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}|^{2}-1=0
, whose unique solution is zero.
8
8

8

One may verify that the coefficients are smooth enough that the
L^{2}
energy estimates make sense.

Finally, when
\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}\in H^{1}_{s}
with
|\bm{X}_{s}^{\rm in}|=1
, we use an approximation argument.
9
9

9

For example, mollify
\bm{X}
and rewrite in arclength parameterization.

∎

3.3.
Energetics and global well-posedness

In this section, we use the energy identity (
2.42
) satisfied by the curve evolution to prove global well-posedness. Defining

(3.62)

E(t)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds\,,

we recall from (
2.42
) that
E
satisfies

(3.63)

\frac{d}{dt}E(t)=-\int_{I}|(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}|^{2}-\gamma\int_{I}|(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}\cdot\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\,.

The right-hand side energy dissipation appears in terms of a quantity

(3.64)

\bm{Z}:=\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}\,.

In particular, defining

(3.65)

D(t)=\int_{I}|\bm{Z}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\,,

we note that

(3.66)

\frac{d}{dt}E(t)\leq-D(t)\,,

and a key point will be to extract control on
\bm{X}_{ssss}
from

(3.67)

\bm{Z}_{s}=\bm{X}_{ssss}-(\lambda\bm{X}_{s})_{s}\,.

Proposition 3.5
.

In the free end setting,
\bm{X}_{ssss}
may be controlled by the energy
E(t)
and dissipation
D(t)
as

(3.68)

\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\lesssim D(t)^{1/2}(1+E(t)^{1/2})+E(t)^{5/2}\,.

In the closed loop setting,
\bm{X}_{ssss}
may be controlled by
E(t)
and
D(t)
as

(3.69)

\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\lesssim D^{1/2}(t)(1+E^{1/2}(t))+E(t)+E(t)^{5/2}\,.

Proof.

One useful observation (cf.
[
52
, p. 406]
) is that it is often geometrically advantageous to work with the quantity
\bm{Z}
instead of
\bm{Z}_{s}
.
We begin by noting the following tangential and non-tangential projections

(3.70)

\displaystyle P_{\bm{X}_{s}}\bm{Z}

\displaystyle=-(|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}+\lambda)\bm{X}_{s}\,,

\displaystyle P_{\bm{X}_{s}}^{\perp}\bm{Z}

\displaystyle=\bm{X}_{sss}+|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\bm{X}_{s}\,,

as well as

(3.71)

\displaystyle P_{\bm{X}_{s}}\bm{Z}_{s}

\displaystyle=(\bm{X}_{ssss}\cdot\bm{X}_{s})\bm{X}_{s}-\lambda_{s}\bm{X}_{s}=-\frac{3}{2}\big(|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\big)_{s}\bm{X}_{s}-\lambda_{s}\bm{X}_{s}\,,

\displaystyle P_{\bm{X}_{s}}^{\perp}\bm{Z}_{s}

\displaystyle=\bm{X}_{ssss}+\frac{3}{2}\big(|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\big)_{s}\bm{X}_{s}\,.

We now consider the free end and closed loop cases separately.

Free ends
. To begin, we recall the boundary conditions
\bm{X}_{ss}\big|_{s=0,1}=\bm{X}_{sss}\big|_{s=0,1}=0
. In particular,
\bm{X}_{ss}\in H^{2}_{0}
, so various homogeneous interpolation inequalities are valid, for example,

(3.72)

\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}\,,

(3.73)

\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}\,.

We follow the observation of
[
85
]
. Since
\bm{Z}
vanishes at the filament endpoints, we may write

(3.74)

\bm{Z}(s)=\int_{0}^{s}\bm{Z}_{s^{\prime}}(s^{\prime})\,ds^{\prime}\,,

and we obtain

(3.75)

\|\bm{Z}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim D(t)^{1/2}\,.

We estimate the tangential component of
\bm{Z}
as

(3.76)

\|\lambda+|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}=\|\bm{Z}\cdot\bm{X}_{s}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim D(t)\|\bm{X}_{s}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim D(t)^{1/2}(1+E(t)^{1/2})\,,

so that, by the triangle inequality, the normal component satisfies

(3.77)

\|P^{\perp}_{\bm{X}_{s}}\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim D(t)^{1/2}(1+E(t)^{1/2})\,.

To estimate the full
H^{1}_{s}
norm of
\bm{X}_{sss}
, it remains to estimate the tangential contribution
P_{\bm{X}_{s}}\bm{X}_{sss}=-|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\bm{X}_{s}
in
H^{1}_{s}
. First,

(3.78)

\||\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\leq\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{4}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{7/8}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/8}\,,

where we use interpolation (
L^{4}
is “halfway” between
L^{2}
and
L^{\infty}
, and then we apply (
3.73
) and (
3.72
)). Second, we may write

(3.79)

(|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\bm{X}_{s})_{s}=2(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss})\bm{X}_{s}+\underbrace{|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\bm{X}_{ss}}_{=-(\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{s})\bm{X}_{ss}}\,,

and therefore

(3.80)

\|(|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\bm{X}_{s})_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/2}\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{3/2}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{5/4}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{3/4}\,.

Thus,

(3.81)

\displaystyle\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\|(P_{\bm{X}_{s}}^{\perp}\bm{X}_{sss})_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}+\|(|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\bm{X}_{s})_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\|(P_{\bm{X}_{s}}^{\perp}\bm{X}_{sss})_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}+C\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{5/4}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{3/4}\,.

Finally, we apply (
3.77
) to the first term and split the second term via Young’s inequality to obtain (
3.68
). This controls
\bm{X}_{ssss}
in
L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{s}
.

Closed loop
. We must now be careful with the mean of
\bm{Z}
, cf.
[
52
, p. 428]
:

(3.82)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bm{Z}\,ds=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})\,ds=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\lambda_{s}\big(\bm{X}-\bm{X}(0)\big)\,ds\,.

Using (
3.71
), we may write

(3.83)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bm{Z}\,ds=-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\bigg(\bm{Z}_{s}\cdot\bm{X}_{s}+\frac{3}{2}\big(|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\big)_{s}\bigg)\big(\bm{X}-\bm{X}(0)\big)\,ds\leq D(t)^{1/2}+E(t)\,,

and hence

(3.84)

\|\bm{Z}\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim D(t)^{1/2}+E(t)\,.

We can now continue with the same arguments as above. All the interpolation inequalities are valid because
\bm{X}_{ss}
has zero mean, and we obtain the conclusion (
3.69
). ∎

In conclusion,
\bm{X}_{s}
belongs to
L^{\infty}_{t}H^{1}_{s}\cap L^{2}_{t}H^{3}_{s}
on its maximal time of existence. Together with the continuation criterion, this proves global well-posedness.

3.4.
Propagation estimates and decay to equilibrium

3.4.1.
Poincaré inequality for free ends

In the free end setting, the dissipation
D(t)
directly controls the energy through the following Poincaré-type inequality
[
85
]
:

(3.85)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:muckinaboutwiththecurvature}}{\leq}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{\{|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}>0\}}|P_{\bm{e}_{\rm n}}\bm{X}_{sss}|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}|\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\leq\frac{1}{\pi^{4}}D(t)\,,

obtained by applying the
H^{1}_{0}(0,1)
Sobolev embedding twice.

The term
|P_{\bm{e}_{\rm n}}\bm{X}_{sss}|^{2}
in (
3.88
) arises in the following way: Let
\kappa^{2}:=|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}
and examine the (at most countably many) open intervals
I_{j}
on which
\kappa^{2}>0
. Since
\kappa^{2}
vanishes to at least second order at
\partial I_{j}
,
\kappa:=\sqrt{|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}}\in C^{1}(\overline{I_{j}})
and is Lipschitz on
[0,1]
. Then

(3.86)

\int_{0}^{1}\kappa^{2}\,ds\leq\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}|\kappa_{s}|^{2}\,ds=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\sum_{j}\int_{I_{j}}|\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{e}_{\rm n}|^{2}\,ds

where
\bm{e}_{\rm n}:=\bm{X}_{ss}/\kappa
is well defined on each interval
I_{j}
.
10
10

10

The less involved computation

(3.87)

\int_{0}^{1}|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds\leq\int_{0}^{1}|\partial_{s}|\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}|\,ds\leq 2\int_{0}^{1}|\bm{X}_{sss}\cdot\bm{X}_{ss}|\,ds

achieves a worse constant.

In summary,

(3.88)

E\leq\frac{1}{2\pi^{4}}D(t)\,.

Let
c_{0}^{-1}
be the best constant in the inequality (
3.88
); in particular,
c_{0}\geq 2\pi^{4}
.
11
11

11

As remarked in
[
85
]
,
\pi^{4}\approx 97
is not the bottom eigenvalue of
-\partial_{s}^{4}
with clamped boundary conditions (the linearized operator around a straight rod), which is approximately
500
. However,
\pi^{4}

is
the bottom eigenvalue of
-\partial_{s}^{4}
with boundary conditions
u,\,\partial_{s}^{2}u|_{s=0,1}=0
.
Combining this with the energy balance, we obtain

(3.89)

\frac{dE}{dt}\leq-D(t)\leq-c_{0}E\,.

Therefore, Grönwall’s inequality implies

(3.90)

E(t)\leq e^{-c_{0}t}E^{\rm in}\,.

Additionally, we may obtain a time-integrated decay estimate on
D
. For
\theta\in(0,1)
, the bound (
3.89
) may be rewritten as

(3.91)

\frac{dE}{dt}+\theta D\leq-(1-\theta)D\leq-c_{0}(1-\theta)D\,,

which, using an integrating factor, may be further rewritten as

(3.92)

\frac{d}{dt}(Ee^{c_{0}(1-\theta)t})+\theta De^{c_{0}(1-\theta)t}\leq 0\,.

Integrating in time yields the estimate

(3.93)

\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{c_{0}(1-\theta)t}D(t)\,dt\leq E^{\rm in}\,.

3.4.2.
Propagation estimates

In the free end setting, we already have the exponential decay estimate (
3.90
), whereas in the periodic setting, we only have
a priori
bounds on the energy and a time-integrated decay of the energy dissipation:

(3.94)

\sup_{t}E(t)\leq E^{\rm in}\,,\quad\int_{0}^{\infty}D(t)\,dt\leq E^{\rm in}\,.

In both the free-end and periodic settings, our goal is to prove pointwise-in-time bounds on the energy dissipation
D(t)
. In the periodic setting, these will be upgraded to a full proof of convergence to Euler elasticae using the Lojasiewicz inequality from Section
6
.

Proposition 3.6
.

In the closed loop setting,

(3.95)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}D(t)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds+C(E)D(t)\times D(t)+C(E)D(t)\,.

In the free end setting,

(3.96)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}D(t)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds+e^{-c_{0}t}\big(C(E)D(t)^{2}+C(E)D(t)\big)+CD(t)+CD(t)^{2}\,.

Proof.

We begin with some calculations, valid for both the free and periodic cases. First, we have

(3.97)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}D(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{I}|\bm{Z}_{s}|^{2}+\gamma(\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\bm{Z}_{s})^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle=\int_{I}[(\mathbf{I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s})\bm{Z}_{s}]\cdot\partial_{t}\bm{Z}_{s}\,ds+\int_{I}\gamma(\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\bm{Z}_{s})(\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\bm{Z}_{s})\,ds\,.

Since
\partial_{t}\bm{Z}|_{s=0,1}=0
in the free end setting, we may write

(3.98)

\int_{I}[\underbrace{(\mathbf{I}+\gamma\bm{X}_{s}\otimes\bm{X}_{s})\bm{Z}_{s}}_{-\partial_{t}\bm{X}}]\cdot\partial_{t}\bm{Z}_{s}\,ds=\int_{I}\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\partial_{t}\bm{Z}=\int_{I}\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\partial_{t}(\bm{X}_{sss}-\lambda\bm{X}_{s})\,ds\,.

The first term produces an advantageous dissipation-type term:

(3.99)

\int_{I}\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{sss}\,ds=-\int_{I}|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds\,.

The second term becomes

(3.100)

-\int_{I}\lambda|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\,,

as the term containing
\partial_{t}\lambda
vanishes due to
\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\bm{X}_{s}=0
. Since
\lambda
may not have an advantageous sign, it must be estimated as

(3.101)

\left|\int_{I}\lambda|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}|^{2}\,ds\right|\lesssim\|\lambda\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\,.

The remaining term in (
3.97
) satisfies

(3.102)

\left|\int_{I}\gamma(\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\bm{Z}_{s})(\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\cdot\bm{Z}_{s})\,ds\right|\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\,.

We proceed with estimating (
3.101
) and (
3.102
) by considering the periodic and free-end cases separately.

Closed loop
.
The tension term (
3.101
) may be estimated by using that the tension satisfies

(3.103)

\|\lambda\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{sss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\,.

We apply an interpolation inequality

(3.104)

\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\,,

since
\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}
has zero mean. Eventually, the term
\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}
will be “broken off” via Young’s inequality, so it remains to estimate

(3.105)

\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\,.

Using the estimates of Proposition
3.5
on
\bm{X}_{ssss}
, the above is estimated by

(3.106)

C(E)D(t)\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}+C(E)\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\leq C(E)D(t)\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}+C(E)D(t)\,.

We will use a Grönwall argument on the quantity
\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}
, which is equivalent to
D(t)
. We will crucially exploit that
D(t)
is already known to be time-integrable to control the coefficients in the Grönwall argument and the “forcing”
C(E)D(t)
.

We now estimate the term (
3.102
) involving
\gamma
. Since
\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}
, we can break off this term, and it remains to estimate
\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{4}
, namely, by
D(t)^{2}
.

Summarizing, we have

(3.107)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}D(t)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds+C(E)D(t)\times D(t)+C(E)D(t)\,,

where the
1/2
on the right-hand side comes from absorbing terms.

Free ends
.
In the free end setting, we may estimate the tension term (
3.101
) by using that

(3.108)

\|\lambda\|_{H^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\,,

due to the boundary conditions. Additionally, we have

(3.109)

\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}+\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}\,.

After breaking off the
\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}
term, it remains to estimate

(3.110)

\|\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\|\bm{X}_{ssss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\,,

as before. However, we already know that
E(t)
is exponentially decaying. Therefore, this term is estimated by

(3.111)

e^{-c_{0}t}(C(E)D(t)^{2}+C(E)D(t))\,.

Next, we estimate the term (
3.102
) involving
\gamma
. For this, we use

(3.112)

\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\lesssim\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/4}\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}\|_{H^{2}_{s}}^{3/4}\lesssim\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}+\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{1/4}\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{3/4}\,.

Then

(3.113)

\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}_{s}}\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{3}+\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{9/4}\|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{3/4}\,.

We break off the
\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}
term to obtain

(3.114)

\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{3}+\|\bm{Z}_{s}\|_{L^{2}_{s}}^{18/5}\lesssim D(t)+D(t)^{2}\,.

Finally, we have

(3.115)

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}D(t)\leq-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}|\partial_{t}\bm{X}_{ss}|^{2}\,ds+e^{-c_{0}t}\big(C(E)D(t)^{2}+C(E)D(t)\big)+D(t)+D(t)^{2}\,.

∎

3.4.3.
Decay to equilibrium

We now leverage the differential inequalities in Proposition
3.6
to prove convergence to equilibrum. First, we establish the following behavior of
E
and
D
:

Proposition 3.7
.

In the closed loop setting,

(3.116)

E(t)\leq E^{\rm in}\,,\quad E(t)\searrow\overline{E}=:\liminf_{t^{\prime}\to+\infty}E(t^{\prime})\text{ as }t\to+\infty

(3.117)

D(t)\to 0\text{ as }t\to+\infty\,.

In the free end setting, we have the refined convergence

(3.118)

E(t)\leq e^{-c_{0}t}E^{\rm in}

(3.119)

D(t)\leq e^{C(E^{\rm in},\theta)}e^{-\theta c_{0}t}[D^{\rm in}+C(E^{\rm in},\theta)]\,,\quad\forall\theta\in[0,1)\,.

Proof.

Closed loop
.
The inequalities at our disposal are

(3.120)

\frac{dE}{dt}=-D\leq 0

(3.121)

\frac{dD}{dt}\leq AD+B

where
A(t)=B(t)=C(E)D(t)
and
\int_{0}^{+\infty}A(t)\,dt\leq C(E^{\rm in})
(the same inequality holds for
B
). Grönwall’s inequality yields

(3.122)

D(t)\leq e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}A(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}}D(t_{0})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}e^{\int_{t^{\prime}}^{t}A(\tau)\,d\tau}B(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\,,\quad\forall t\geq t_{0}\geq 0\,.

In particular,

(3.123)

D(t)\leq e^{C(E^{\rm in})}D(t_{0})+e^{C(E^{\rm in})}C(E^{\rm in})\int_{t_{0}}^{t}D(t^{\prime})\,dt^{\prime}\,.

Since
D(t)\in L^{1}_{t}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we can choose a sequence of initial times
t_{0,k}\to+\infty
such that
D(t_{0,k})\to 0
, thereby demonstrating (
3.117
).

Notably, if we choose
t_{0}=0
in (
3.123
), then we obtain upper bounds on
D
which are exponential in
E^{\rm in}
(more specifically, of the type
e^{C\times(E^{\rm in})^{p}}
for some
p\geq 1
).

Free end
. In this case, we have the refined inequalities

(3.124)

\frac{dE}{dt}=-D\leq-c_{0}E

(3.125)

\frac{dD}{dt}\leq-c_{0}D+e^{-c_{0}t}C(E^{\rm in})(D^{2}+D)+C(D^{2}+D)+c_{0}D\,,

where have added and subtracted
c_{0}D
. We already have integrated exponential decay (
3.93
) for
D
, which we will upgrade to pointwise exponential decay. For short times,
D
is controlled by the exponential-in-
E^{\rm in}
bound (
3.123
) with
t_{0}=0
. However, by the integral estimates (
3.93
) on
D
, we have the following: For any
\theta>0
